IVAC Researchers Inform Global Guidance on COVID-19 Vaccines - Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health

News Brief
Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health's International Vaccine Access Center (IVAC) researchers contributed to the World Health Organization's (WHO) first-ever position paper on COVID-19 vaccines, released in 2026. The paper summarizes essential background information on the disease and vaccine performance, offering data-backed recommendations for national immunization programs. The research, funded by the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI), evaluated over 5,000 studies assessing vaccine effectiveness across diverse populations, including older adults, immunocompromised individuals, children, and pregnant individuals. The findings indicate that the vaccine remains highly effective at preventing hospitalizations and deaths, with no identified group where it does not work. The paper emphasizes routine vaccination for high-risk populations and suggests that countries may consider it for others based on context-specific factors such as cost-effectiveness. The recommendations are supported by systematic reviews of global evidence and are available in an interactive database for public health decision-makers.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Connect With Us Make A Gift Search Who We Are Our Work Resources News Skip Section Navigation Breadcrumb Home IVAC Researchers Inform Global Guidance on COVID-19 Vaccines IVAC Researchers Inform Global Guidance on COVID-19 Vaccines Published August 26, 2026 COVID-19 Vaccines WHO recently released its first-ever position paper on COVID-19 vaccines, which summarizes essential background information on COVID-19 disease as well as vaccine performance and safety. The paper provides data-backed recommendations for the use of COVID-19 vaccines in national immunization programs. The sections on immunogenicity, efficacy, and effectiveness were informed by work from a team of International Vaccine Access Center (IVAC) faculty that has been systematically reviewing data on COVID-19 vaccines since 2021. In a project funded by the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI), IVAC researchers evaluated more than 5,000 studies assessing COVID-19 vaccine effectiveness in different populations, including older adults, immunocompromised individuals, persons with comorbidities, children, healthy adults, and pregnant individuals and infants. “Our team has been tracking global evidence on COVID-19 vaccine performance since early 2021 to inform recommendations along the way,” noted Melissa Higdon, MPH, the project’s lead investigator. “This position paper focuses on the most recent evidence for the currently available vaccines.” The team’s findings have been made available in an interactive database to provide public health decision-making authorities, researchers, and industry stakeholders with the latest vaccine effectiveness data. Since COVID-19 has shown a determination to stick around, COVID-19 vaccines should now be considered part of the routine immunization landscape of the future. – Maria Deloria Knoll, project co-lead “Since we’ve been doing this since the beginning, we have a sense of everything that has happened in the past, allowing us to interpret evolving conditions and methodologies and how that affects estimates of effectiveness,” said study co-lead Maria Deloria Knoll, PhD, IVAC Director of Epidemiology and a Research Professor at the Bloomberg School of Public Health. “We have found that the COVID-19 vaccine has remained highly effective at preventing hospitalizations and deaths. We don’t really see a group that it doesn’t work in.” Routine vaccination is recommended for groups at highest risk of severe COVID-19 disease, while countries may consider routine vaccination for other populations depending on context-specific factors such as cost-effectiveness or programmatic feasibility. The recommended timing for COVID-19 vaccination depends on local disease epidemiology, though co-administering COVID-19 vaccine and influenza vaccine may facilitate uptake by reducing the number of required health care visits. Those at highest risk for severe disease outcomes should receive at least 1 dose per year, though 2 doses administered 6 months apart is preferred. “Since COVID-19 has shown a determination to stick around, COVID-19 vaccines should now be considered part of the routine immunization landscape of the future,” said Deloria Knoll. COVID-19 - Britannica

It confirms that the virus was first detected in Wuhan, China, in 2019 and has since spread globally, causing millions of deaths. The disease is primarily transmitted through respiratory droplets and contaminated surfaces, with higher risk for older adults and individuals with chronic illnesses. The article highlights the impact of the pandemic on society, including the shift to remote work and education, and discusses the use of vaccines, antiviral drugs, and other preventive measures to mitigate the spread and severity of the disease. The response includes public health strategies such as social distancing, surface disinfection, and face mask usage, as well as the development of vaccines and therapeutic monoclonal antibodies. The article does not provide specific details on the current status of the pandemic but emphasizes the ongoing efforts to manage its effects.

COVID-19 was first detected in 2019 in Wuhan, China. A large proportion of infections in China were undocumented before travel restrictions and other control measures were implemented in late January 2020. As a result, COVID-19 very quickly spread to countries worldwide, giving rise to a multiyear pandemic that resulted in millions of deaths. The pandemic also spurred a revolution in business and education, spawning a reliance on distance learning and work-at-home arrangements and the rise of videoconferencing platforms such as Zoom, which became one of the most downloaded applications worldwide and a household word. Read more about the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. COVID-19 is caused by a coronavirus known as severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2). The disease is transmitted primarily through contact with infectious material, particularly respiratory droplets that enter the environment when an infected person sneezes or coughs. Individuals nearby may inhale or come into contact with these droplets, resulting in disease transmission. Infection may also occur when a person comes into contact with a contaminated surface and then touches his or her mouth, nose, or eyes. Individuals at greatest risk of COVID-19 infection include older adults and persons with chronic illness, largely because of weakened immune function. Britannica Quiz A Visit with the Word Doctor: Medical Vocabulary Quiz COVID-19 is characterized by a variety of symptoms, including fever, cough, congestion, fatigue, shortness of breath, headache, sore throat, nausea or vomiting, loss of smell or taste, and body aches. COVID-19 may progress to severe respiratory illness, with symptoms of chest pain and extreme difficulty in breathing, requiring hospitalization. Some COVID-19 patients who are hospitalized further develop neurological symptoms, including severe fatigue and altered consciousness. Delirium has been observed in many of these patients as well, possibly as a side effect of medication. Delirium and lingering psychological issues, including depression and anxiety, can prolong and complicate recovery. COVID-19 vaccineDoctor holding a syringe and bottle with COVID-19 vaccine.(more) There is no cure for COVID-19. However, different types of drugs have been used to treat infection and to reduce the severity of the disease. Examples include antiviral drugs, such as remdesivir, molnupiravir, and combined ritonavir and nirmatrelvir; a drug used for pancreatic inflammation called camostat mesilate; and various therapeutic monoclonal antibodies, such as REGEN-COV (casirivimab and imdevimab). COVID-19 vaccines, which became available in late 2020, are highly effective in protecting against severe illness and in limiting the spread of the disease; immunity can be further bolstered through subsequent booster doses of vaccine. Risk of disease transmission can be reduced by measures such as social distancing, disinfection of surfaces, and universal community use of face masks. Self-isolation and self-quarantine are other ways in which the spread of COVID-19 can be stopped. Survivors of severe COVID-19, particularly those who were hospitalized, are likely to suffer long-term effects. Individuals who required mechanical ventilation might never fully recover; ventilator use is associated with severe muscle atrophy and weakness, which significantly impact survival and quality of life. How We Develop New VaccinesWorried about diphtheria? No? Thank vaccines.(more) See all videos for this article The ongoing spread of COVID-19 has been fueled by the emergence of variants in evolving lineages of SARS-CoV-2. Such variants generally carry mutations that strengthen characteristics such as the virus’s ability to infect individuals (including individuals who are vaccinated and who previously had COVID-19), to cause severe disease, and to potentially escape certain treatments. There are numerous variants, the most notable of which is Omicron, which was first detected in Botswana in November 2021 and quickly became the primary circulating strain globally. Omicron spawned multiple sublineages of concern, including BA.4 and BA.5, which are highly infectious, and BA.2, which gave rise to XBB.1.5—a highly transmissible variant, noted for its heightened ability to bind to cells and to replicate. The Lasting Effects of COVID-19 on Kids - Children's Hospital Association

The Lasting Effects of COVID-19 on Kids - Children's Hospital Association
新闻简报The Children's Hospital Association published a news article on August 27, 2026, discussing the long-term effects of COVID-19 on children. The article highlights the findings of the Pediatric COVID Outcomes Study (PECOS), a collaboration between Children's National Hospital and the National Institutes of Health, which enrolled over 1,000 children and adolescents. The study identified persistent symptoms such as fatigue, headaches, and cognitive difficulties in some children, with variations by age group. These symptoms often affected children's daily activities and social development. The research also found no evidence of lasting heart problems in children, despite concerns about cardiac damage. The study emphasizes the need for coordinated care among different medical specialties due to the complexity of long-term symptoms. While most children recover completely, those who do not require further attention and support. The findings aim to help clinicians and families better understand and address the challenges faced by children with long COVID.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The study was conducted during the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic, with the publication date being 2026-08-27. The research followed children over time, with some symptoms persisting months or years after infection.
地点与对象
The study was conducted in the United States, focusing on children and adolescents. It included a carefully screened group of over 1,000 participants who had recovered from their initial COVID-19 infection and a comparison group that had never been infected.
事件
A comprehensive pediatric study, the Pediatric COVID Outcomes Study (PECOS), was conducted to examine the long-term effects of COVID-19 on children. The study identified persistent symptoms in some children, including fatigue, headaches, and cognitive difficulties, and highlighted the challenges in diagnosing and treating these cases.
规模、影响与风险
研究发现,与未感染儿童相比,康复儿童中有20种症状更为常见。这些症状包括疲劳、头痛、胃肠道问题、持续咳嗽和认知困难。研究还指出,部分症状随时间改善,而另一些症状则持续存在或延迟出现。研究强调,由于这些症状的多样性和复杂性,需要协调护理。
应对、状态与不确定性
The study is ongoing, with additional detailed analysis of EKG and cardiac MRI data in progress. The findings suggest that most children recover completely from COVID-19, but those who do not require further attention and research. The study is a collaboration between Children's National Hospital and the National Institutes of Health.

The Lasting Effects of COVID-19 on Kids - Children's Hospital Association

News Brief

Register for ALC 2026 today. × Advocacy News Resources Events Services About LOG IN AdvocacyNewsResourcesEventsServicesAbout CHAFind a HospitalCareersContact Us Log In Privacy Policy Terms of Use Security Frequently Asked Questions © Children's Hospital Association The Lasting Effects of COVID-19 on Kids Article A physician researcher highlights five takeaways from a comprehensive study on long COVID. Published Aug. 26, 2026 | 4 min. read When the COVID-19 pandemic began, doctors had little information about what recovery would look like for children months or years after infection. Now, one of the largest and most comprehensive pediatric COVID-19 studies is beginning to provide answers. The Pediatric COVID Outcomes Study (PECOS), a collaboration between Children's National Hospital and the National Institutes of Health, enrolled over 1,000 children and adolescents and followed them over time, comparing those who had COVID-19 with a carefully screened group that had never been infected. Unlike COVID outcomes studies that use retrospective reviews of medical records, this prospective study relies on detailed evaluations, physical exams, surveys, and testing of participants in person. The study has produced a series of papers examining long-term symptoms, behavioral health, and cardiac outcomes. Together, they offer one of the clearest pictures yet of COVID-19's lasting effects on children and raise important questions about how children's hospitals care for patients whose symptoms persist long after an infection has resolved. Here are five key takeaways from the research so far, according the co-principal investigator Roberta DeBiasi, MD, chief of pediatric infectious diseases at Children’s National Hospital. 1. Long COVID in children is real The PECOS team identified a group of children who experienced persistent symptoms after COVID-19 infection. Those symptoms varied by age and changed over time, but for some children they affected far more than physical health. Fatigue can make it difficult to get through a school day. Headaches can interfere with concentration. Exercise intolerance can keep children from participating in sports and other activities that are central to childhood. “There is a subset of kids who have symptoms which affect their life,” DeBiasi said. “Particularly when they’re at a time of life when they should be attending school and being with their friends and developing socially and doing sports.” For families searching for answers, those symptoms are often difficult to explain and difficult to connect to a single diagnosis. 2. Long COVID doesn’t look the same in every child Long COVID appears differently across age groups and may evolve over time. Researchers identified 20 symptoms that were more common among children who had recovered from their initial COVID-19 infection than among those who were not infected. Among the most frequently reported: Fatigue Headaches Gastrointestinal problems Persistent cough Difficulties with concentration or memory Muscle and joint pain Trouble sleeping Symptoms seen in younger children — such as prolonged fever or dry persistent cough — often differed from those reported by adolescents and young adults. And some symptoms improved over time while others persisted or appeared later. This vast spectrum of symptom presentation is one reason diagnosing and treating these children can be challenging. 3. The pandemic affected an entire generation’s mental health One finding surprised DeBiasi and her colleagues. Children who had COVID-19 reported more physical symptoms, more pain, more sleep problems, and greater difficulty with cognitive functioning than children who had not been infected. But rates of anxiety and depression were similar in both groups. That suggests many of the mental health challenges children experienced over the past several years were not caused by infection alone. “The pandemic has affected all children,” DeBiasi said. 4. COVID-19 doesn't cause lasting heart problems Parents often worry COVID-19 might leave lasting damage to a child’s heart. Researchers specifically looked for evidence of that and found none. Even children who reported symptoms such as chest pain, fatigue, or racing heartbeats generally had normal cardiac function on advanced testing, including an echocardiogram. Additional detailed analysis of EKG and cardiac MRI data is in progress. For families who continue to worry about long-term heart damage, DeBiasi says these findings should provide reassurance. 5. Children with post-infection symptoms need coordinated care Many of the challenges facing children with long COVID are familiar to clinicians who care for patients with other infection-associated chronic illnesses, including post-treatment Lyme disease syndrome, myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS), PANS/PANDAS, and other conditions that can persist after an infection has resolved. These patients don’t fit neatly into a single specialty. A child may see a cardiologist for chest pain, a neurologist for headaches, a gastroenterologist for abdominal symptoms, and a psychologist for cognitive concerns. Families can spend months moving through the health care system without anyone looking at the whole picture. "We hear over and over from these families that people don't believe them," she said. DeBiasi sees an opportunity to recognize infection-associated chronic illness as a clinical category worthy of dedicated expertise, research, and care models. What comes next? The next challenge, DeBiasi said, is understanding why some children recover quickly while others continue to struggle months or years after infection. Researchers are now using the study’s clinical data and biological samples to search for those answers. In the meantime, DeBiasi hopes the research helps clinicians and families recognize an important reality: While most children recover completely from COVID-19, those who don’t deserve to be heard, supported, and studied. “Resources that are applied to adults should be equally applied to kids,” DeBiasi said. “There’s a specific group of children who need our attention.” COVID-19 Research Data and Trends What's Trending Why the Pediatric Workforce Crisis Demands Federal Action The Alarming Shortage of Pediatricians and 4 Solutions to Break the Cycle 7 Ways Pediatric Care Is Different From Adult Care Reducing Vitamin K Refusals Medicaid: The History of Children's Health Related Content Timing and Texting Improve Vaccination Rates April 07, 2025 Removing Vaccine Barriers for Refugee Children March 28, 2025 Increasing Free Vaccination Access in Rural Counties March 27, 2025

How Long Does Brain Fog Last After COVID-19? Study Offers Answers

How Long Does Brain Fog Last After COVID-19? Study Offers Answers
新闻简报A study published in the journal Neuroprotection investigated cognitive changes in 3,419 patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period in China, between December 2022 and March 2023. Researchers assessed cognitive function six months and two years post-infection, finding that 67.1% of participants maintained stable cognitive function, while 22.0% experienced reversible decline and 10.8% had irreversible decline. Age 60 years or older was linked to a more than fourfold higher risk of progressive decline, as were severe illness, hypertension, longer hospitalization, and white matter lesions. Higher education and more vaccination doses were protective factors. The study suggests that cognitive recovery is common after Omicron infection, but some patients may experience persistent deficits. These findings could help clinicians identify high-risk patients and guide targeted monitoring and rehabilitation strategies. Future studies with longer follow-up and in-person assessments are recommended to further understand cognitive recovery and progression.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究历时两年,于感染后6个月及2年进行评估。发表日期为2026年8月30日。
地点与对象
The study involved 3,419 patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period in China, with assessments conducted across nine hospitals. The study also noted differences in brain fog symptoms among patients in the U.S., India, and Nigeria.
事件
A two-year study tracked cognitive changes in patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period, finding that most patients recovered, while some experienced persistent or progressive cognitive decline. The study was published in the journal Neuroprotection.
规模、影响与风险
在3,419名患者中,67.1%认知功能保持稳定,22.0%出现可逆性下降,10.8%出现不可逆性下降(其中7.2%为非进行性,3.6%为进行性)。年龄≥60岁与超过4倍的进行性认知下降风险相关,重症、高血压、住院时间延长及脑白质病变亦为进行性认知下降的危险因素;受教育程度较高及接种更多剂次疫苗接种疫苗则为保护因素。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究为认知恢复与下降提供见解,建议对高风险患者进行针对性监测与康复。研究基于电话认知状态测试-40(TICS-40)及老年人认知下降信息员问卷(IQCODE)评估。研究仍在进行中,未来需进一步明确2年以上的认知轨迹。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How Long Does Brain Fog Last After COVID-19? Study Offers Answers

News Brief
A study published in the journal Neuroprotection investigated cognitive changes in 3,419 patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period in China, between December 2022 and March 2023. Researchers assessed cognitive function six months and two years post-infection, finding that 67.1% of participants maintained stable cognitive function, while 22.0% experienced reversible decline and 10.8% had irreversible decline. Age 60 years or older was linked to a more than fourfold higher risk of progressive decline, as were severe illness, hypertension, longer hospitalization, and white matter lesions. Higher education and more vaccination doses were protective factors. The study suggests that cognitive recovery is common after Omicron infection, but some patients may experience persistent deficits. These findings could help clinicians identify high-risk patients and guide targeted monitoring and rehabilitation strategies. Future studies with longer follow-up and in-person assessments are recommended to further understand cognitive recovery and progression.
Original source text (English)

Study Offers Answers How Long Does Brain Fog Last After COVID-19? Study Offers Answers Written by Colleen Fleiss Reviewed by The Medindia News Review Team on Aug 30 2026 11:49 PM Can brain fog after Omicron improve? A two-year study found most patients recovered, while some experienced persistent or progressive decline. Cognitive difficulties, commonly referred to as “brain fog,” are a recognized symptom of long COVID and can affect attention, memory and executive function. Previous SARS-CoV-2 variants, including the original strain, Alpha and Delta, have been linked to persistent cognitive impairment. () However, because the Omicron variant generally caused less severe illness, questions remained about whether cognitive problems would persist, improve or worsen over time, particularly among older adults. Omicron Variant of COVID-19 Omicron variant is a mutated strain of coronavirus (SARS-CoV-2) with faster transmissibility and enhanced virulence compared to the original COVID-19 virus. Researchers Examine Two-Year Cognitive Changes After COVID-19 To investigate this, a research team led by Professor Yan Wan and Professor Bo Hu from the Department of Neurology at Union Hospital, Tongji Medical College, Huazhong University of Science and Technology in China, tracked 3,419 patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period between December 2022 and March 2023. The patients were treated across nine hospitals in China. Researchers assessed participants’ cognitive function by telephone six months and two years after infection using the Telephone Interview of Cognitive Status-40 and the Informant Questionnaire on Cognitive Decline in the Elderly. Of the 3,419 patients, 2,087 survivors completed both assessments. Based on changes in cognitive function over time, researchers classified participants into four groups: stable cognition, reversible decline, non-progressive decline and progressive decline. The study was published online in the journal Neuroprotection. Why is Long COVID Brain Fog More Common in High-Income Countries? Long COVID affects people differently from varied geographical locations. Patients in the U.S. reported much more brain fog symptoms than those in India or Nigeria. Study Finds Cognitive Recovery Is Common After Omicron Infection Two years after discharge, 67.1% of participants maintained stable cognitive function, while 22.0% experienced reversible cognitive decline. Irreversible decline affected 10.8%, comprising 7.2% with non-progressive decline and 3.6% with progressive decline. Among participants with reversible decline, TICS-40 scores increased by 3 points from 21.00 at 6 months to 24.00 at 2 years. Improvement occurred in attention and calculation, delayed recall, registration, learning ability, and executive function. By contrast, the progressive group showed a 4-point TICS-40 decrease, from 17.00 to 13.00, with deterioration particularly affecting attention and calculation, delayed recall, and registration. “Our findings indicate that cognitive decline after Omicron infection is not necessarily permanent,” says Prof. Wan. “The substantial recovery observed over 2 years is encouraging, but the persistence of deficits in a vulnerable minority shows why patients at higher risk should not be overlooked.” Age 60 years or older was linked to more than fourfold higher odds of progressive decline, while each additional infection increased the odds more than twofold. Severe COVID-19, hypertension, longer hospitalization, and white matter lesions were also independently associated with progressive decline. In contrast, each additional year of education and a higher number of vaccination doses were protective. These findings could help clinicians target follow-up and rehabilitation resources. Healthcare providers could consider age, infection history, disease severity, vascular health and brain imaging when deciding who needs closer monitoring. The domain-specific results may also inform rehabilitation approaches targeting attention, memory, learning and executive function. For patients and families, evidence that many cognitive changes improve may reduce anxiety while encouraging follow-up for persistent symptoms. Can Acupuncture Give True Relief in Breast Cancer Brain Fog Acupuncture improved cognition in breast cancer patients, with real acupuncture showing greater gains in objective skills like memory and attention than controls. Targeted Monitoring May Help Manage Long-Term Cognitive Effects of COVID “Our results support a more targeted approach to long-term cognitive care after COVID-19,” says Prof. Hu. “Identifying high-risk patients early could help guide monitoring and rehabilitation, while broader prevention measures such as vaccination may reduce the likelihood of progressive decline.” The findings may encourage collaboration among neurologists, rehabilitation specialists, geriatricians and researchers. Future studies could build on these results with longer follow-up and comprehensive, in-person neuropsychological assessments to clarify how cognitive trajectories evolve beyond 2 years. Overall, the study shows that post-Omicron cognitive decline is largely reversible, but recovery is not universal. The findings point toward a balanced long-term strategy: reassure patients that improvement is common, while identifying older adults and others with clinical or imaging risk factors for closer assessment. Over the coming years, risk-based monitoring and targeted rehabilitation could help reduce the cognitive and social burden of COVID-19, while research clarifies pathways determining recovery versus progression. References: Post-Omicron cognitive decline is largely reversible: Two-year multicenter cohort study of domain-specific outcomes in hospitalized patients - (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/nep3.70050) Source-Eurekalert Test your Knowledge on Coronavirus (COVID-19) Coronavirus is a large positive-strand RNA virus that is surrounded by a protein envelope. View five news elements
Time
The study was conducted over a two-year period, with assessments made six months and two years after infection. The publication date is August 30, 2026.
Location and population
The study involved 3,419 patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period in China, with assessments conducted across nine hospitals. The study also noted differences in brain fog symptoms among patients in the U.S., India, and Nigeria.
Event
A two-year study tracked cognitive changes in patients hospitalized with COVID-19 during the Omicron-dominant period, finding that most patients recovered, while some experienced persistent or progressive cognitive decline. The study was published in the journal Neuroprotection.
Scale, impact and risk
Among the 3,419 patients, 67.1% maintained stable cognitive function, 22.0% experienced reversible decline, 10.8% had irreversible decline (7.2% non-progressive and 3.6% progressive). Age 60 years or older was linked to more than fourfold higher odds of progressive decline, while severe illness, hypertension, longer hospitalization, and white matter lesions were also associated with progressive decline. Higher education and more vaccination doses were protective factors.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study provides insights into cognitive recovery and decline post-Omicron infection, suggesting targeted monitoring and rehabilitation for high-risk patients. The findings are based on assessments using the Telephone Interview of Cognitive Status-40 and the Informant Questionnaire on Cognitive Decline in the Elderly. The study is ongoing, with future research needed to clarify cognitive trajectories beyond 2 years.
